A_{-j,-j}
is potentially singular. Third, there is no guarantee that
\overline{A}
will be symmetric when
A
is symmetric. Therefore, we have to compute the full matrix
\overline{A}
even if
A
is known to be symmetric.
Finally, notice that Algorithm
2
does not require us to solve (
23
), which is possibly non-convex, since a stationary point of (
23
) suffices. In Section
10.4
, we will demonstrate how to choose the tuning parameters
\lambda^{nl}_{j}
and
B_{j}
.

Remark 10.1
(Possible other choices of
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
).

The de-biasing literature borrows nodewise lasso from precision matrix estimation literature. Other methods for precision matrix estimation, e.g. Constrained

l_{1}
-minimization for Inverse Matrix Estimation aka CLIME
(
Cai et al. 2011
)
, the graphical lasso aka GLASSO
(
Friedman et al. 2008
)
etc. may also be used in place of nodewise lasso to construct
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
provided Condition
4.1
is satisfied under realistic structural assumptions. In this regard,
CLIME solves convex optimization problems and has fast implementation. It has also seen application in context of de-biasing
(
Neykov et al. 2018
)
.
We conjecture that if the columns of
\Phi^{0}
are bounded in
l_{1}
norm, then the CLIME estimator satisfies the desired Condition
4.1
as well. This requirement, however, is stricter than that of the nodewise lasso; see Assumption
10.4
in Section
10.4
. To keep our discussions focused, we refrain from further discussion on the asymptotics of CLIME here.
Similar to CLIME, GLASSO also has fast implementation and is widely used in precision matrix estimation. However, the current literature lacks results supporting its consistency. There is, instead, some evidence against its asymptotic convergence to the precision matrix, at least in
l_{\infty}
norm
(
Mazumder & Hastie 2012
)
.

10.2
Intuition behind the nodewise lasso algorithm

To provide an intuition behind why Algorithm
2
works, we argue that if the input matrix
A
in Algorithm
2
is positive definite, the algorithm outputs
A^{-1}
when the penalty parameters
\lambda^{nl}_{j}
’s are set to zero. To that end, we first invoke a standard linear algebra result
(
Rao & Bhimasankaram 2000
, cf.)
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Suppose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
A
is an
m\times m
positive definite matrix. Then
A_{-j,-j}
is invertible for
j=1,\ldots,m
. Moreover,

(A^{-1})_{j,j}=\dfrac{1}{A_{j,j}-A_{j,-j}^{T}A_{-j,-j}A_{-j,j}}

(A^{-1})_{-j,j}=-(A^{-1})_{j,j}(A_{-j,-j})^{-1}A_{-j,j}.

Defining
\eta_{j}=(A_{-j,-j})^{-1}A_{-j,j}
, we note that

\argmin_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}}(\eta^{T}A_{-j,-j}\eta-2A_{-j,j}^{T}\eta)=\eta_{j},\quad j=1,\ldots,m.

Also, in parallel with (
2
),
we define

\Gamma_{j}=(-(\eta_{j})_{1},\ldots,-(\eta_{j})_{j-1},1,-(\eta_{j})_{j+1},\ldots,-(\eta_{j})_{r-1}),

.
Then it follows that

\tau_{j}^{2}=\Gamma_{j}^{T}A\Gamma_{j}=A_{j,j}-A_{j,-j}^{T}A_{-j,-j}A_{-j,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}1/(A^{-1})_{j,j},

(26)

where (a) follows from Lemma
10.2
.
Applying Lemma
10.2
again, we can show that the
j
th element of the output matrix
\overline{A}
equals

\Gamma_{j}/\tau_{j}^{2}=(A^{-1})_{j}.

(27)

10.3
Nodewise lasso for our case

In this section, we discuss the finite sample properties of our nodewise lasso estimator
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
. We begin with some implications of
the discussion in Section
10.2
for the special case when the input matrix
A=H^{0}
. First, note that, in this case, for
j=1,\ldots,p+q
,

\eta_{j}^{0}=\argmin_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}}\Big(\eta^{T}H^{0}_{-j,-j}\eta-2(H^{0}_{-j,j})^{T}\eta\Big)

satisfies

\eta_{j}^{0}=(H^{0}_{-j,-j})^{-1}H^{0}_{-j,j}.

(28)

Moreover, (
26
) implies for
j=1,\ldots,p+q
,

(\tau^{0}_{j})^{2}=H^{0}_{j,j}-(H^{0}_{j,-j})^{T}H^{0}_{-j,-j}H^{0}_{-j,j}

(29)

satisfies
(\tau^{0}_{j})^{2}=(\Phi^{0}_{j,j})^{-1}
. From (
27
), it then follows that

(\Phi^{0})_{-j,j}=-\eta_{j}^{0}/(\tau^{0}_{j})^{2}.

(30)

Because
\widehat{\eta}_{j}
is a stationary point of (
23
), it satisfies
the KKT condition, which takes the form

-2A_{j,-j}+2A_{-j,-j}\widehat{\eta}_{j}+\lambda^{nl}_{j}\partial\|\widehat{\eta}_{j}\|_{1}=0,

where
\lambda^{nl}_{j}
is as in (
23
) and
\partial\|\widehat{\eta}_{j}\|_{1}
is the partial derivative of the
l_{1}
norm evaluated at
\widehat{\eta}_{j}
.
It then follows that
(
Janková & van de Geer 2018
, cf. Section 3.1 of )

A_{j}^{T}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}=\widehat{\tau}_{j}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|A_{-j}^{T}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{j}^{nl}/2

(31)

A^{T}\overline{A}-I_{p+q}|_{\infty}=O\Big(\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\lambda^{nl}_{j}/{\widehat{\tau}}_{j}^{2}\Big).

10.4
Asymptotic properties of the nodewise lasso estimator

In this Section, we will show that the nodewise lasso estimator satisfies Condition
4.1
under some regulatory conditions. We will go through these regulatory conditions first.

Recall from (
28
) the definition of
\eta_{j}^{0}
.
We will require the number of non-zero elements in
\eta_{j}^{0}
, i.e.
\|\eta_{j}^{0}\|_{0}
, to be small, which is in parallel with
Janková & van de Geer 2018
.

{assumption}
[ Assumption on the column sparsity of
\Phi^{0}
]

\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\|{\eta^{0}}_{j}\|_{0}=O(s)
, where
s=s_{U}+s_{V}
.

Since
\absolutevalue{\|\eta^0_j\|_0- \|\Phi^0_j\|_0}\leq 1
by (
30
), a restriction on
\|\eta^{0}_{j}\|_{0}
actually induces a restriction on
\|\Phi^{0}_{j}\|_{0}
, which explains the nomenclature of Assumption
10.4
.

Assumption
10.4
can be hard to decipher, and it may be hard to verify. Therefore we will now give a sufficient condition for Assumption
10.4
.
Lemma
21.1
in Supplement
21.1

gives the explicit form of
\Phi^{0}
, which indicates that

\|{\eta^{0}}_{j}\|_{0}\leq s_{U}+s_{V}+\|({\Sigma}_{x})_{j}^{-1}\|_{0}+\|({\Sigma}_{y})_{j}^{-1}\|_{0}\quad(j=1,\ldots,p).

Therefore,
we only require the column sparsities of
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
to be
O(s)
for Assumption
10.4
is satisfied. This sparsity requirement is formulated as Condition
10.4
.

{condition}

[ A sufficient condition for Assumption
10.4
]

The maximum number of non-zero elements per column of
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}
or
{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
is
O(s)
.

Sparsity restriction on the columns of
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
is more intuitive than sparsity restriction on
\Phi^{0}
. It is a well known fact that
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}
or
{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
is sparse if the partial correlation between the
X_{i}
’s or the
Y_{i}
’s are mostly zero, which may be satisfied when only a few of these variables interact among themselves. The latter is not unusual in high dimensional genomic data because genes, proteins etc. generally form clusters.
Such sparsity restrictions are also common in the literature; cf.
Bühlmann & Van De Geer 2011
;
Janková & van de Geer 2018
.

Now we are ready to state the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 10.3
(Nodewise lasso Theorem).

Suppose Assumptions
2.1
,
2.1
,
4.1
,
10.4
hold, and the preliminary estimators
\widehat{x}_{n}
and
\widehat{y}_{n}
satisfy Condition
4.1
. Further suppose

\|\eta_{j}^{0}\|_{1}\leq B_{j}\leq C_{T}s^{1/2}\quad(j=1,\ldots,p+q)

for some
C_{T}>0
. Then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
depending on
C_{T}
such that for

\lambda^{nl}_{j}=C\lambda,

the estimator
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
obtained by feeding
\widehat{H}_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})
to Algorithm
2
satisfies Condition
4.1
. here
\lambda
is as in (
4
).

The nodewise lasso estimator
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
depends on
\widehat{x}_{n}
and
\widehat{y}_{n}
via
\widehat{H}_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})
, which does not rely on the sign of
\widehat{x}_{n}
and
\widehat{y}_{n}
. Hence, the asymptotics of
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
, unlike the de-biased estimators, is unaffected by the sign flip of
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{n}
.

11
Connection to Related Literature

The study of asymptotic inference in the context of SCCA naturally connects to the popular research direction of de-biased/de-sparsified inference in high dimensional models
(
Zhang & Zhang 2014
;
Javanmard & Montanari 2014
;
van de Geer et al. 2014
;
Janková & van de Geer 2018
;
Zhang & Zhang 2014
;
Ning et al. 2017
;
Neykov et al. 2018
;
Janková & van de Geer 2017
;
Janková & Van De Geer 2016
;
Cai et al. 2017
;
Mitra et al. 2016
;
Bellec & Zhang 2019
)
.
This line of research, starting essentially from the seminal work of
Zhang & Zhang 2014
, more or less follows the general prescription laid out in Section
3.1
.
Similar to our case, these methods also often depend on potentially high dimensional parameters – and thereby require initial good estimators of them.
For example, asymptotically valid confidence interval for the coordinates of a sparse linear regression vector relies critically on good initial estimators of the regression vector and nuisance parameter in form of the precision matrix of the covariates
(
Zhang & Zhang 2014
;
Javanmard & Montanari 2014
;
van de Geer et al. 2014
)
.

The construction of a suitable estimating equation is however somewhat case specific, and can be involved based on the nature of the high dimensional nuisance parameters. Since SCCA involves a list of high dimensional nuisance parameters including the covariance matrices
{\Sigma}_{x}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}
, special attention is required in deriving our inferential procedures.

Among the above-mentioned methods, our approach bears the greatest resemblance to the method recently espoused by
Janková & van de Geer 2018
in the context of Sparse Principal Component Analysis (SPCA). However, there are substantial differences between
Janková & van de Geer 2018
’s approach and ours.
First, due to the presence of high dimensional nuisance parameters
{\Sigma}_{x}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}
, the canonical correlation analysis problem in general is more complicated than the principal component analysis problem
(
Gao et al. 2015
;
Gao et al. 2017
)
. Thus, blindly following
Janková & van de Geer 2018
works neither for the SCCA part nor for the actual de-biasing step. Second, Section
3.1
indicates that the correct choice of the objective function
f
is crucial to any de-biasing method.
Janková & van de Geer 2018
’s objective function bases on the well-known fact that the first principal component extraction problem can be written as an unconstrained Frobenius norm minimization problem. No such analogue, to the best of our knowledge, was previously available in the CCA literature. We had to construct a novel objective function whose unconstrained optimization yields the first canonical directions; see Lemma
3.2
.
Third,
Janková & van de Geer 2018
applies the de-biasing procedure on some preliminary estimator, similar to us. However, their preliminary estimators are based on solving a penalized version of the non-convex principal component analysis optimization problem. To aid the computation, the authors restrict the search space to a small neighborhood of a consistent estimator of the first principal component. The said consistent estimator is found by semi-definite programming. They also show that, any stationary point of the resulting optimization program consistently estimates the first principal component. This removes the burden of finding the global minima, but the program still remains non-convex. Our SCCA method, on the other hand, is inspired by
Gao et al. 2017
’s approach, where the non-convex optimization part is replaced by a lasso.

12
On the Conditions and Assumptions of Section
4

In this section we provide a detailed discussions on assumptions made for the sake of theoretical developments in Section
4.1
.

Discussion on Condition
4.1

First, some remarks are in order regarding the range of
\kappa\in[1/2,1]
in Condition
4.1
. Theorem 3.2 of
Gao et al. 2017
implies that it is impossible for
\kappa
to be strictly less than
1/2
since the minimax rate of the
l_{2}
error is roughly
s^{1/2}\lambda
under Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
2.1
. If
\kappa
is larger, i.e.
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{n}
have slower rates of convergence, and we pay a price in terms of the sparsity restriction
s=o(n^{1/(4\kappa)}(\log(p+q))^{-1/(2\kappa)})
in Assumption
4.1
.
Supplement
9
shows that estimators satisfying Condition
4.1
with
\kappa=1
exist. In fact, most SCCA estimators with theoretical guarantees have
l_{2}
error guarantee of
s^{\kappa}\lambda
with
\kappa\in[1/2,1]
. The interested reader can refer to
Gao et al. 2017
;
Gao et al. 2015
;
Chen et al. 2013
and references therein. Subsequently, in view of the above, we let
\kappa\in[1/2,1]
.

In light of Condition
4.1
, indeed
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{n}
with faster rate of convergence, i.e.
\kappa=1/2
, is preferable.
COLAR and
Chen et al. 2013
’s estimator attain this minimax rate when
r=1
. We do not yet know if there are SCCA estimators which attain the minimax rate for
r>1
while only estimating the first canonical direction. For
r>1
, the estimation problem becomes substantially harder because the remaining
r-1
canonical directions start acting as high dimensional nuisance parameters. It is likely that a trade-off between computational and estimation efficiency arises in presence of these additional nuisance parameters. In particular, it is plausible that
the minimax rate of
\kappa=1/2
may not be achievable by polynomial time algorithms in this case. To gather intuition about this, it is instructive to look at the literature on estimating the first principal component direction in high dimensions under sparsity. In this case, to the best of our knowledge, polynomial time algorithms attain the minimax rate only in the single spike model, or a slightly relaxed version of the latter. We refer the interested reader to
Wang et al. 2016
for more details.

The algorithms that do succeed to estimate the first principal component under multiple spikes at the desired minimax rate attempt to solve the underlying non-convex problem, and hence are not immediately clear to be polynomial time
(
Yuan & Zhang 2013
;
Ma et al. 2013
;
Janková & van de Geer 2018
)
. In this case,
Yuan & Zhang 2013
and
Ma et al. 2013
’s methods essentially reduce to power methods that induce sparsity by iterative thresholding.
Chen et al. 2013
’s method tries to borrow this idea in context of SCCA in the rank one case; see Remark
9.3
for a discussion on the problems that their method may face in presence of nuisance canonical directions.

Finally for the inferential question, it is natural to consider an extension of ideas from sparse PCA as developed in
(
Janková & van de Geer 2018
)
. When translated to SCCA, their approach will aim to solve

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{p},y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}{\mathrm{subject~to}}$}][l]{\underset{\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{p},y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}{\mathrm{minimize}}}\quad\widehat{h}_{n}(x,y)+C\lambda(\|x\|_{1}+\|y\|_{1})\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle,

(32)

where
C>0
is a constant, and

\widehat{h}_{n}(x,y)=(x^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x)^{2}/2+(y^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}y)^{2}/2-2x^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}y.

We conjecture that for a suitably chosen
C
, the resulting estimators will satisfy Condition
4.1
with
\kappa=1/2
. However, (
32
) is non-convex and solving (
32
) is computationally challenging for large
p
and
q
. Analogous to
Janková & van de Geer 2018
, one can simplify the problem by searching for any stationary point of (
32
) over a smaller feasible set, namely a small neighborhood of a consistent preliminary estimator of
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
. However, while this first stage does guarantee a good initialization, the underlying optimization problem still remains non-convex. Since the aim of the paper is efficient inference of
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
whose computational efficiency is theoretically guaranteed, we stick with the modified COLAR estimators and refrain from exploring the above-mentioned route.

Discussion on Assumption
4.1
:

It is natural to wonder whether the condition
s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2}=o(n^{-1/2})
is at all necessary, especially since it is much stricter than
s\lambda=o(1)
, which is sufficient for the
l_{2}
consistency of
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{n}
presented in Theorem
9.1
of Supplement
9
. However,
current literature on inference in high dimensional sparse models bears evidence that the restriction
s\lambda^{2}=o(n^{-1/2})
might be unavoidable.
In fact, this sparsity requirement is a staple in most de-biasing approaches whose preliminary estimators are minimax optimal, including sparse principal component analysis
(
Janková & van de Geer 2018
)
and sparse generalized linear models
(
van de Geer et al. 2014
;
Javanmard & Montanari 2014
)
.
Indeed, in case of sparse linear regression,
Cai et al. 2017
shows that this sparsity is necessary for adaptive inference. We believe similar results hold for our case as well. However, further enquiry in that direction is beyond the scope of the present paper.

Next, it is natural to ask why Assumption
4.1
involves sparsity restriction not only on
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
, but also on the other columns of
U
and
V
.
This restriction stems from the initial estimation procedure of
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
. Although we estimate only the first pair of canonical directions,
the remaining canonical directions act as nuisance parameters.
Thus, to efficiently estimate
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
, we need to separate the other covariates from
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
. Therefore, we need to estimate the other covariates’ effect efficiently enough. Consequently we require some regularity assumptions on these nuisance parameters as precisely quantified by Assumption
4.1
.

Discussion on Condition
4.1
:

This is a standard assumption in de-biasing literature in that similar assumptions have appeared in sparse PCA
(
Janková & van de Geer 2018
)
and sparse generalized linear models literature
(
van de Geer et al. 2014
)
– both of whom use the nodewise lasso algorithm to construct
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
.
We remark in passing that that
Javanmard & Montanari 2014
’s construction of de-biased lasso does not require the analogue of
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
, which is the precision matrix estimator in their case, to satisfy any condition like Condition
4.1
. Instead, it requires
(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}
’s to be small. It is unknown whether such constructions work in the more complicated scenario of CCA or PCA.

13
Proof Preliminaries

This section states the facts and lemmas that are used repeatedly in the proofs. The proofs are deferred to Section
22
unless they are very trivial.

First, we derive some results for
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{n}
satisfying Condition
4.1
.

Lemma 13.1
.

Suppose
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{n}
satisfy Condition
4.1
. Further suppose Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
4.1
hold.
Then

\|\widehat{\rho}_{n}|-{\rho}_{0}|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda).

Moreover

\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-1=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\beta}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}\widehat{\beta}_{n}-1=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)

Recall that we have defined

\widehat{x}_{n}=|\widehat{\rho}_{n}|^{1/2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n},\quad\widehat{y}_{n}=|\widehat{\rho}_{n}|^{1/2}\widehat{\beta}_{n},\quad x^{0}=({\rho}_{0})^{1/2}\alpha_{0},\quad y^{0}=({\rho}_{0})^{1/2}\beta_{0}.

The following lemma gives the rates of
\widehat{x}_{n}
and
\widehat{y}_{n}
when Condition
4.1
holds.

Lemma 13.2
.

Under the set up of Lemma
13.1
,

\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}+\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda)

and

\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}+\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)

where
\kappa
is as defined in (
22
).

The following lemma entails that
\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}
and
\widehat{y}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}\widehat{y}_{n}
consistently estimate
{\rho}_{0}^{2}
.

Lemma 13.3
.

Under the set up of Lemma
13.1
, we have

\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-{\rho}_{0}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)\quad\widehat{y}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}\widehat{y}_{n}-{\rho}_{0}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)

where
\kappa
is as defined in(
22
).

Proof 13.4
(of Lemma
13.3
).

Noting

|\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-{\rho}_{0}|\leq|\widehat{\rho}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-1)|+\|\widehat{\rho}_{n}|-{\rho}_{0}|,

the proof follows from Lemma
13.1
and the fact
|\widehat{\rho}_{n}|\leq 1
. The proof for
\widehat{y}_{n}
follows in a similar way.

Now we state an implication of Assumption
4.1
.

Fact 1
.

Suppose
\lambda
is as in (
4
).
Then Assumption
4.1
implies
s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda=o(1)
and
s\lambda=o(1)
.

Now we state some linear algebra facts.

Fact 2
.

For any two matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, we have

\|P_{A}-P_{B}\|_{F}^{2}=\text{rank}(A)+\text{rank}(B)-2\text{tr}(P_{A}P_{B}),

where
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
are the projection matrices onto the column spaces of
A
and
B
, respectively.

Proof 13.5
.

Noting
P_{A}^{2}=P_{A}
and
P_{B}^{2}=P_{B}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|P_{A}-P_{B}\|_{F}^{2}=\text{tr}\Big((P_{A}-P_{B})^{T}(P_{A}-P_{B})\Big)=\text{tr}(P_{A})+\text{tr}(P_{B})-2\text{tr}(P_{A}P_{B}),

from which the result follows because for projection matrix
P_{A}
,
\text{tr}(P_{A})=\text{rank}(A)
.

Fact 3
.

For any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
,
\|A\|_{F}\leq r^{1/2}\|A\|_{op}

Proof 13.6
(of Fact
3
).

Suppose
\varsigma_{i}
’s are the singular values of
A
. Then

\|A\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\varsigma_{i}^{2}\leq r\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\varsigma_{i}^{2}.

Therefore
\|A\|_{F}\leq r^{1/2}\|A\|_{op}
.

Fact 4
(Lemma 2.1.3 of
Chen et al. 2020
).

Suppose
A
and
B
are two matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
. Then

2^{-1/2}\|P_{A}-P_{B}\|_{F}\leq\inf_{W\in\mathcal{O}(r,r)}\|AW-B\|_{F}\leq\|P_{A}-P_{B}\|_{F}

Fact 5
.

Suppose
x
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Then

\|P_{x}-P_{y}\|_{F}\leq 4\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\frac{\|wx-y\|_{2}}{\max(\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2})}.

The next lemma shows that the
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
norms of
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
are bounded.

Lemma 13.7
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, we have

\|\alpha_{0}\|_{1},\|\beta_{0}\|_{1}\leq(Ms)^{1/2},

where
s=s_{U}+s_{V}
.
Also

\|\alpha_{0}\|_{2},\|\beta_{0}\|_{2}\leq M^{1/2},

where
M
is as in Assumption
2.1
.

Proof 13.8
.

Since

\|\alpha_{0}\|^{2}_{2}\Lambda_{min}({\Sigma}_{x})\leq|\alpha_{0}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\alpha_{0}|
, we have
\|\alpha_{0}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{M}
by the
2.1
Assumption. Similarly,
\|\beta_{0}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{M}.

Now, Cauchy Schwartz inequality implies

\|\alpha_{0}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{s}\|\alpha_{0}\|_{2}=\sqrt{Ms}.

The same can be proved for
\beta_{0}
, which completes the proof.

Now we state some rate-results for Sub-Gaussian covariance matrices.

Lemma 13.9
.

Suppose
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
are sub-Gaussian matrices.
Then there exists constant
C
depending only on the subgaussian parameter of
(X,Y)
so that

|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x}|_{\infty},|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y}|_{\infty},|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy}|_{\infty}\leq C\lambda

with high probability as
n,p,q\to\infty
.
Moreover, for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, there exists constant
C
depending only on the subgaussian parameter of
(X,Y)
so that

\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})v\|_{\infty}\leq C\|v\|_{2}\lambda\quad\text{and}\quad\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})v\|_{\infty}\leq C\|v\|_{2}\lambda.

with high probability as
n,p,q\to\infty
.

Lemma 13.10
.

Let
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and the set
S\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
has cardinality
s
. Suppose
v
satisfies the cone condition
\|v_{S^{c}}\|_{1}\leq C^{\prime}\|v_{S}\|_{1}
for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
and
S
has cordinality
s
. Then under the set up of Lemma
13.9
, there exists
C>0
depending only on the subgaussian parameter of
X
and
C^{\prime}
so that

|v^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})v|\leq Cs\|v\|_{2}^{2}\lambda,

with high probability as
n,p,q\to\infty
.

Proof 13.11
.

Noting

\|v\|_{1}\leq(C^{\prime}+1)\|v_{S}\|_{1}\leq(C^{\prime}+1)s^{1/2}\|v_{S}\|_{2}
, we obtain

\absolutevalue{v^T(\hSx-\Sx)v}\leq\|v\|_{1}^{2}|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x}|_{\infty}\leq(C^{\prime}+1)^{2}s\|v\|_{2}^{2}|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x}|_{\infty}.

Then the proof follows by Lemma
13.9
.

Lemma 13.12
.

Supppose
X
is sub-Gaussian and a random vector
\widehat{z}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfies
\|\widehat{z}_{n}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{1/2})
, where
s
satisfies
\lambda^{1/2}s=o(1)
. Then there exists
C
depending only on the sub-gaussian parameters of
X
so that

|\widehat{z}_{n}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})\widehat{z}_{n}|\leq C(s^{1/2}\lambda\|\widehat{z}_{n}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\widehat{z}_{n}\|_{1})

with high probability for sufficiently large
n,p
, and
q
.

Lemma 13.13
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are sub-Gaussian and random vectors
\widehat{z}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\widehat{w}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
satisfy

\|\widehat{z}_{n}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{w}_{n}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{1/2}),

where
s
satisfies Assumption
4.1
. Then there exists
C
depending only on the subgaussian parameters of
X
and
Y
so that

|\widehat{z}_{n}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})\widehat{w}_{n}|\leq C\lambda\Big(s^{1/2}(\|\widehat{z}_{n}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\widehat{w}_{n}\|_{2}^{2})+(\|\widehat{z}_{n}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{w}_{n}\|_{1})\Big)

with high probability as
n,p,q\to\infty
.

Lemma 13.14
(Lemma 8 of
Janková & van de Geer 2018
).

Suppose
X
is sub-Gaussian and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a vector with
\|z\|_{0}=s
. Then

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{z^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})z}{\|z\|^{2}_{2}}=O_{p}((s\log p/n)^{1/2}).

Lemma 13.15
.

Suppose
\widehat{z}_{n}
is a random vector, possibly depending on
X
, so that
\|\widehat{z}_{n}-z_{0}\|_{1}=o_{p}(1)
where
z_{0}
is a fixed vector with finite
l_{2}
norm. Then depending only on the subgaussian parameter of
X
so that

|x^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})\widehat{z}_{n}|\leq C\|z_{0}\|_{2}\|x\|_{1}O_{p}(\lambda)

with high probability as
n,p,q\to\infty
.

Our next result is on multivariate normal distribution. This result quite well known and can be obtained via straightforward calculation.

Fact 6
(Fourth moments of multivariate normal distribution).

Suppose
X\sim N_{p}(0,{\Sigma}_{x})
. Then

E[X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}]=({\Sigma}_{x})_{11}({\Sigma}_{x})_{22}+2({\Sigma}_{x})_{12}^{2},

E[X_{1}^{3}X_{2}]=3({\Sigma}_{x})_{11}({\Sigma}_{x})_{12},

E[X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}]=({\Sigma}_{x})_{12}({\Sigma}_{x})_{34}+({\Sigma}_{x})_{13}({\Sigma}_{x})_{24}+({\Sigma}_{x})_{14}({\Sigma}_{x})_{23}.

The next result gives an expression for the variance of quadratic terms of multivariate Gaussian random vectors.

Fact 7
.

Suppose

(X,Y)\sim N_{p+q}(0,\Sigma)\quad\text{where}\quad\Sigma=\begin{bmatrix}{\Sigma}_{x}&{\Sigma}_{xy}\\
{\Sigma}_{yx}&{\Sigma}_{y}\end{bmatrix}.

Further suppose
a,b,z\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Then it follows that

\text{var}(a^{T}XX^{T}b)=(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}a)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)+(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)^{2}\ \text{and}\ \text{var}(z^{T}XY^{T}d)=(z^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}z)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}d)+(z^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)^{2}.

The next fact is regarding the sub-exponential norms of quadratic forms in
X
and
Y
.

Fact 8
.

Suppose
a,c\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Then sub-Gaussian random vectors
X
and
Y
satisfy

\|a^{T}XY^{T}b\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}},\quad\|a^{T}XX^{T}c\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\|a\|_{2}\|c\|_{2}\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}.

Next, we present a result on Gaussian random vectors.

Lemma 13.16
.

Suppose

(X,Y)\sim N_{p+q}(0,\Sigma)\quad\text{where}\quad\Sigma=\begin{bmatrix}{\Sigma}_{x}&{\Sigma}_{xy}\\
{\Sigma}_{yx}&{\Sigma}_{y}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
a,b,z\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
c,d,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
be some deterministic vectors. Then

\displaystyle T=a^{T}XX^{T}b+c^{T}YY^{T}d-z^{T}XY^{T}d-b^{T}XY^{T}\gamma

has variance

\displaystyle(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}a)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)+(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)^{2}+(c^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}c)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}d)+(c^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}d)^{2}

\displaystyle\ +(z^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}z)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}d)+(z^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)^{2}+(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)(\gamma^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}\gamma)+(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}\gamma)^{2}

\displaystyle\ +2(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}c)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)+2(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}c)+2(z^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}\gamma)+2(z^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}\gamma)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)

\displaystyle\ -2(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}z)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)-2(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}d)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}z)-2(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}\gamma)-2(a^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}\gamma)(b^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}b)

\displaystyle\ -2(c^{T}{\Sigma}_{yx}z)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}d)-2(c^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}d)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{yx}z)-2(c^{T}{\Sigma}_{yx}b)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}\gamma)-2(c^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}\gamma)(d^{T}{\Sigma}_{yx}b).

The next fact is a result obtained using the delta method.

Fact 9
.

Suppose

n^{1/2}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta\\
\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-\vartheta\end{bmatrix}\to_{d}N_{2}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\sigma^{2}_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{21}&\sigma^{2}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\right)

where the covariance matrix is positive definite and
\theta\neq 0
.
Then

n^{1/2}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{1/2}\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-\theta^{1/2}\vartheta)\to_{d}N\bigg(0,\frac{\vartheta^{2}\sigma_{11}^{2}}{4\theta}+\sigma_{22}^{2}\theta+\vartheta\sigma_{12}\bigg).

Proof 13.17
(of Fact
9
).

The proof follows by delta method. Let us denote
f(x,y)=x^{1/2}y
. Then the gradient of
f
writes as
\gradient f(x,y)=(x^{-1/2}y/2,x^{1/2})
. Observe that

\gradient f(x,y)^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\sigma^{2}_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{21}&\sigma^{2}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\gradient f(x,y)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{x^{-1/2}y}{2}&x^{1/2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sigma^{2}_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{21}&\sigma^{2}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x^{-1/2}y/2\\
x^{1/2}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{y^{2}\sigma_{11}^{2}}{4x}+\sigma_{22}^{2}x+y\sigma_{12}

is positive if
y>0
.

Note that since
\theta\neq 0
,
\gradient f(\theta,\vartheta)
is non-zero. Therefore, an application of delta method establishes that

n^{1/2}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{1/2}\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-\theta^{1/2}\vartheta)
is asymptotically centered normal with variance

\frac{\vartheta^{2}\sigma_{11}^{2}}{4\theta}+\sigma_{22}^{2}\theta+\vartheta\sigma_{12}.

14
Proof of Lemmas in Section
3

In this section, we prove the lemmas from Section
3
.

Proof 14.1
(of Lemma
3.2
).

Suppose
A={\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1/2}
.
Denoting
\tilde{U}={\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U
and
\tilde{V}={\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}V
, we observe that
\tilde{U}\in\mathcal{O}(p,r)
and
\tilde{V}\in\mathcal{O}(q,r)
, where the latter sets are defined in (
17
). Hence,
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Lambda_{i}\tilde{u}_{i}\tilde{v}_{i}^{T}=\tilde{U}\Lambda\tilde{V}^{T}
is a singular value decomposition of
A
. Let us also define
A_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Lambda_{i}\tilde{u}_{i}\tilde{v}_{i}^{T}
.
When
k=1
. Then from (
5
) it can be shown that

\argmin_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}}\|A-xy^{T}\|_{F}^{2}=\{(c_{1}\tilde{u}_{1},c_{2}\tilde{v}_{1}):c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\ c_{1}c_{2}=\Lambda_{1}\}.

(33)

From (
33
) we deduce that for any
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(c_{1}\tilde{u}_{1},c_{2}\tilde{v}_{1})
is a solution to

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}}{\mathrm{subject~to}}$}][l]{\underset{\displaystyle(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}}{\mathrm{minimize}}}\quad\|A-xy^{T}\|_{F}^{2}\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle

(34)

as long as
c_{1}c_{2}=\Lambda_{1}={\rho}_{0}
. Thus, there is an infinite set of minimizers of (
34
).
Since
\|\tilde{u}_{1}\|_{2}=\|\tilde{v}_{1}\|_{2}=1
, if we add the additional constraint
x^{T}x=y^{T}y
to (
34
), its only minimizers are
\pm({\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\tilde{u}_{1},{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\tilde{v}_{1})
.
More precisely, for any
C>0
we have

\pm({\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\tilde{u}_{1},{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\tilde{v}_{1})=\argmin_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p},y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\bigg\{\|A-xy^{T}\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{C}{4}(x^{T}x-y^{T}y)^{2}\bigg\}.

Because
{\Sigma}_{x}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}
are positive definite,
the reparametrization
x\mapsto{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}x
and
y\mapsto{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}y
yields

\displaystyle\pm({\rho}_{0}^{1/2}{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}\tilde{u}_{1},{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1/2}\tilde{v}_{1})=\argmin_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p},y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\bigg\{\|A-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{C}{4}(x^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x-y^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y)^{2}\bigg\}.

(35)

Finally, noting

\tilde{u}_{1}={\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\alpha_{0}
and
\tilde{v}_{1}={\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\beta_{0}
, we see that the left hand side of (
35
) equals
\pm({\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\alpha_{0},{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\beta_{0})
.
Hence, for any
C>0

({\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\alpha_{0},{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\beta_{0})=\argmin_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p},y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\bigg\{\|A-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{C}{4}(x^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x-y^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y)^{2}\bigg\}.

A little algebra shows

\displaystyle\|A-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \langle A-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2},A-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \langle A,A\rangle-2\langle{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2},A\rangle+(x^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x)(y^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle A,A\rangle-2Tr({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1/2}{\Sigma}_{yx}xy^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2})+(x^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x)(y^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \langle A,A\rangle-2x^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}y+(x^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x)(y^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y)

Since the minimizers do not depend on
\langle A,A\rangle
, the proof follows by elementary algebra.

Proof 14.2
(of Lemma
3.4
).

Let us denote
\tilde{u}_{i}={\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}u_{i}

and
\tilde{v}_{i}={\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}v_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
.
Letting
D=\text{Diag}({\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2},{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2})
, and recalling
x^{0}={\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\alpha_{0}
and
y^{0}={\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\beta_{0}
, we rewrite
H^{0}
in (
7
) as

\displaystyle H^{0}=2{\rho}_{0}D\begin{bmatrix}I_{p}+2\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{u}_{1}^{T}&-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1/2}/{\rho}_{0}\\
-{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1/2}{\Sigma}_{yx}{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}/{\rho}_{0}&I_{q}+2\tilde{v}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}^{T}\end{bmatrix}D.

(36)

Let us consider

A=\begin{bmatrix}I_{p}+2\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{u}_{1}^{T}&-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\Lambda V^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}/{\rho}_{0}\\
-{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}V\Lambda U^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}/{\rho}_{0}&I_{q}+2\tilde{v}_{1}\tilde{v}_{1}^{T}\end{bmatrix}.

If we can show that
\Lambda_{min}(A)>0
then it will follow that
A
is invertible, implying

H^{0}(x^{0},y^{0})^{-1}=(2{\rho}_{0})^{-1}D^{-1}A^{-1}D^{-1},

leading to

\|H^{0}(x^{0},y^{0})^{-1}\|_{op}\leq(2{\rho}_{0})^{-1}\|D^{-1}\|_{op}\|A^{-1}\|_{op}\|D^{-1}\|_{op}

which, combined with Assumption
2.1
, yields

\Lambda_{min}(H(x^{0},y^{0}))^{-1}\leq\frac{M\Lambda_{min}(A)^{-1}}{2{\rho}_{0}}.

Therefore,

\Lambda_{min}(H(x^{0},y^{0}))\geq 2{\rho}_{0}\Lambda_{min}(A)/M.

(37)

Therefore, it suffices to find a lower bound on
\Lambda_{min}(A)
. To that end, first note that since
\{\tilde{u}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{u}_{r}\}
is a set of orthogonal vectors, they can be extended to an orthogonal basis
\{\tilde{u}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{u}_{r},\tilde{u}_{r+1},\ldots,\tilde{u}_{p}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Similarly, we can extend

\{\tilde{v}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{v}_{r}\}
to an orthogonal basis
\{\tilde{v}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{v}_{r},\tilde{v}_{r+1},\ldots,\tilde{v}_{q}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Let us consider

z=(\tilde{u}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i})
for
2\leq i\leq r
. Since
\tilde{u}_{i}^{T}\tilde{u}_{1}=\tilde{v}_{i}^{T}\tilde{v}_{1}=0
, and

Az=\begin{bmatrix}(1-\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0})\tilde{u}_{i}\\
(1-\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0})\tilde{v}_{i}\end{bmatrix}=(1-\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0})z.

Thus
z
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue
1-\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0}
. A similar case is when
z=(\tilde{u}_{i},-\tilde{v}_{i})
. Then

Az=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{u}_{i}+\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0}\tilde{u}_{i}\\
-\tilde{v}_{i}-\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0}\tilde{v}_{i}\end{bmatrix}=(1+\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0})z.

In this case also
Az=z
with eigenvalue
1-\Lambda_{i}/{\rho}_{0}
.
Now suppose
z=(\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{v}_{1})
. Then
Then
Az=2z
, implying it is an eigenvector with eigenvalue 2. Now consider
z=(\tilde{u}_{1},-\tilde{v}_{1})
. Then
Az=4z

which implies
z
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue 4. Therefore, we have obtained
2r
orthogonal eigenvectors of
A
.
Next, consider
z=(\tilde{u}_{i},0)
where
r+1\leq i\leq p
. Then
U^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{u}_{i}=0
as well as
\tilde{u}^{T}\tilde{u}_{i}=0
. Hence
Az=z
, i.e.
z
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue 1.
Similarly,
z=(0,\tilde{u}_{j})
for
r+1\leq j\leq q
is also an eigenvector of
A
with eigenvalue one. Therefore, we have obtained total
2r+(p-r)+(q-r)=p+q
many orthogonal eigenvectors of
A
with non-zero eigenvalues. and
\Lambda_{min}(A)=1-\Lambda_{2}/{\rho}_{0}
. Therefore the current lemma follows from (
37
).

15
Proof of Theorem
4.1

15.1
Preliminaries for the proof of Theorem
4.1

We keep using the notations defined in the earlier sections. Especially, recall the
\lambda
defined in (
4
). Several times we will use the followings without stating which holds by Condition
4.1
:

\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\|(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{j}-\Phi^{0}_{j}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda),

and

\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\|(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{j}-\Phi^{0}_{j}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda).

Note that Lemma
13.2
implies

\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}+\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda),

\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}+\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda),

and Lemma
13.1
implies
|\widehat{\rho}_{n}-{\rho}_{0}|=O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda)
. It turns out that if
\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}
and
\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}
are small, then
(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n})_{i}-{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}(\alpha_{0})_{i}
is asymptotically normal for
1\leq i\leq p
, but if
\|\widehat{x}_{n}+x^{0}\|_{1}
and
\|\widehat{x}_{n}+x^{0}\|_{2}
are small, then
(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n})_{i}+{\rho}_{0}^{1/2}(\alpha_{0})_{i}
will be asymptotically normal. An analogous result holds for
\widehat{y}^{db}_{n}
and
\beta_{0}
. Therefore, there can be four different scenarios depending on whether
\widehat{x}_{n}
or
\widehat{y}_{n}
has a sign flip. Since the proofs for all these cases are identical, we will only consider the case when
\widehat{x}_{n}
and
\widehat{y}_{n}
are aligned with
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
, i.e.

\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}=\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1},\quad\|\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{1}=\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{1},

and

\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}=\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2},\quad\|\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{2}=\inf_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|w\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{2}.

Therefore, we will have

\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda),\quad\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda).

The following fact follows immediately from the above:

\displaystyle\|\widehat{x}_{n}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{1/2}),\ \|\widehat{x}_{n}\|_{2}=O_{p}(1),\ \|\widehat{y}_{n}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{1/2}),\ \|\widehat{y}_{n}\|_{2}=O_{p}(1).

(38)

Suppose
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.
Recall the definitions of
\partial\widehat{h}_{n}/\partial x
and
\partial\widehat{h}_{n}/\partial y
from (
3.2
). Also, recall from (
3.2
) that when
C=2
,

\displaystyle{\partialderivative{h}{x}}(x,y)=2(x^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}y){\Sigma}_{x}x-2{\Sigma}_{xy}y

\displaystyle{\partialderivative{h}{y}}(x,y)=2(y^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y){\Sigma}_{y}y-2{\Sigma}_{yx}x.

For the sake of brevity, we will use the notations

\gradient h(x,y)=\begin{bmatrix}{\partialderivative{ h}{x}}(x,y)\\
{\partialderivative{ h}{y}}(x,y)\end{bmatrix},\quad\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(x,y)=\begin{bmatrix}{\partialderivative{\widehat h_n}{x}}(x,y)\\
{\partialderivative{\widehat h_n}{y}}(x,y)\end{bmatrix}.

Notice also that

\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})=0
when
x^{0}={\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\alpha_{0}
and
y^{0}={\rho}_{0}^{1/2}\beta_{0}
.

15.2
Proof architecture

Now we will start the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
From Definiton
9
, we find the decomposition

\displaystyle-\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}\\
\widehat{y}^{db}_{n}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}x^{0}\\
y^{0}\end{bmatrix}=

\displaystyle\ \Phi^{0}\Big(\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})\Big)

\displaystyle\ +(\widehat{\Phi}_{n}^{T}-\Phi^{0})\Big(\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\widehat{\Phi}_{n}^{T}\Big(\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})\Big)+\begin{bmatrix}x^{0}-\widehat{x}_{n}\\
y^{0}-\widehat{y}_{n}\end{bmatrix}.

Here we used the fact that
\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})=0
. The above decomposition
indicates for
1\leq i\leq p
,

\displaystyle x^{0}_{i}-(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2\underbrace{(\Phi^{0}_{i})^{T}\begin{bmatrix}((x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})y^{0}+((x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}){\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}\\
((y^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}y^{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})x^{0}+((y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}){\Sigma}_{y}y^{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{T_{1}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{((\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}-\Phi^{0}_{i})^{T}(\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0}))}_{T_{2}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}^{T}\bigg(\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})+\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})\bigg)}_{T_{3}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}^{T}\Big(\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})-H^{0}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\\
\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\end{bmatrix}\Big)}_{T_{4}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\Big(e_{i}^{T}-(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}^{T}H^{0}\Big)\begin{bmatrix}x^{0}-\widehat{x}_{n}\\
y^{0}-\widehat{y}_{n}\end{bmatrix}}_{T_{5}(i)}

The term
T_{1}(i)
is the main contributing term in that it is asymptotically equivalent to
\mathcal{L}_{i}
. We will prove the theorem in two steps. The first step shows that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{1}(i)-\mathcal{L}_{i}|=O_{p}(s\lambda^{2})

and
n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{i}
converges weakly to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
4\sigma^{2}_{i}
.
The last four steps show that the remaining terms are asymptotically negligible, i.e.

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{k=2}^{5}|T_{k}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2}).

Because
s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2}=o(n^{-1/2})
, the proof follows.

As in Section
4
, we denote
\Phi^{0}_{i}=((\Phi^{0}_{i})_{1},(\Phi^{0}_{i})_{2})
where
(\Phi^{0}_{i})_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
(\Phi^{0}_{i})_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.
For notational convenience, we will denote
\Phi^{0}_{i,1}=(\Phi^{0}_{i})_{1}
and
\Phi^{0}_{i,2}=(\Phi^{0}_{i})_{2}
. Similarly we define
\widehat{\Phi}_{i,1}
and
\widehat{\Phi}_{i,2}
so that
(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}=(\widehat{\Phi}_{i,1},\widehat{\Phi}_{i,2})
. We drop the
n
from the subscript of
\widehat{\Phi}_{n}
for the sake of simplicity.
The following fact, which follows from Lemma
20.1
and Assumption
2.1
, will be used repeatedly:

\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\max\{\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2},\|\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}\}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\|\Phi^{0}_{i}\|_{2}\leq\|\Phi^{0}\|_{op}\leq\frac{M}{2({\rho}_{0}-\Lambda_{2})}\leq\frac{M}{2\epsilon_{0}}.

(39)

15.3
Step 1: showing the asymptotic normality of
T_{1}(i)

We can split
T_{1}(i)
into two terms:

\displaystyle T_{1}(i)=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{2(\Phi^{0}_{i})^{T}\begin{bmatrix}{\rho}_{0}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})y^{0}+((x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}){\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}\\
{\rho}_{0}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})x^{0}+((y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}){\Sigma}_{y}y^{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{2(\Phi^{0}_{i})^{T}\begin{bmatrix}((x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}-{\rho}_{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\\
((y^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}y^{0})-{\rho}_{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\end{bmatrix}}_{T_{12}(i)}

Note that the second term
T_{12}
is bounded by

\displaystyle|(x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}|+|(y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\|(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}|.

By Lemma
13.14
it follows that

|(x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}|=\|x^{0}\|^{2}_{2}O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda).

From Lemma
13.15
it follows that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}|=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|x^{0}\|_{1}O_{p}(\lambda).

From Lemma
20.1
it follows that there exists
C>0
so that
\|\Phi^{0}\|_{op}\leq C
. Therefore,

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|(\Phi^{0})_{i}\|_{2}\leq\|\Phi^{0}\|_{op}\leq C.

From Lemma
13.7
it also follows that
\|x^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(1)
and
\|x^{0}\|_{1}=O(s^{1/2})
. Thus

|(x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}|=O_{p}(s\lambda^{2}).

Similarly we can show that

|(y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\|(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}|=O_{p}(s\lambda^{2}).

Thus, we conclude
We have established in (
40
) that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{1}(i)-\mathcal{L}_{i}|=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{12}(i)|=O_{p}(s\lambda^{2}).

(40)

15.4
Step 2: Showing
T_{2}(i)
is small

We have

\displaystyle\gradient\widehat{h}_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})=

\displaystyle\ 2\begin{bmatrix}{\rho}_{0}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})y^{0}+((x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}){\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}\\
{\rho}_{0}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})x^{0}+((y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}){\Sigma}_{y}y^{0}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\ +\begin{bmatrix}((x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}-{\rho}_{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\\
((y^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}y^{0})-{\rho}_{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\end{bmatrix}.

Lemma
13.7
and
13.14
imply that

(x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}=O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda)\quad\text{and}\quad(y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}=O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda).

Therefore using Assumption
2.1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle|T_{2}(i)|\leq

\displaystyle\ \|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,1}-\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{1}\Big(\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\|_{\infty}+\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})y^{0}\|_{\infty}+\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\|_{\infty}O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda)\Big)

\displaystyle\ +M\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,1}-\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|x^{0}\|_{2}O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda)

\displaystyle\ +\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,2}-\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{1}\Big(\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\|_{\infty}+\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})x^{0}\|_{\infty}+\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\|_{\infty}O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda)\Big)

\displaystyle\ +M\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,2}-\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}\|y^{0}\|_{2}O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda).

Now note that

\displaystyle\max\{\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,1}-\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{1},\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,2}-\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{1}\}\leq\|(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}-\Phi^{0}_{i}\|_{1}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda)

(41)

and

\displaystyle\max\{\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,1}-\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2},\|\widehat{\Phi}_{i,2}-\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}\}\leq\|(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}-\Phi^{0}_{i}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)

(42)

by Condition
4.1
.
Also by Lemma
13.7
, Lemma
13.9
, and Lemma
13.9
,

\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}\|_{\infty},\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})y^{0}\|_{\infty},\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}\|_{\infty},\|(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})x^{0}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(\lambda).

Therefore,

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{2}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2}+s^{\kappa+1}\lambda^{3}).

15.5
Step 3: Showing
T_{3}(i)
is asymptotically negligible

For any vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, consider the partition
(z_{1},z_{2})=z
where
z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
. When
z=\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})+\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})
, we derive the expression

\displaystyle 2^{-1}\bigg(\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(x^{0},y^{0})-\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})+\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})\bigg)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n})\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}(\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0})+2\Big(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}\Big)\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}

\displaystyle\ -\bigg\{(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{x}_{n}){\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})-{\Sigma}_{xy}(\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0})+\Big(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}\Big){\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})+\Big(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})\widehat{x}_{n}\Big){\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})

\displaystyle\ -(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})(\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0})+(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0})(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}

\displaystyle\ +\Big(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}-\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{x}_{n}+(x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}\Big){\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}.

Using Assumption
2.1
we obtain that

\displaystyle 2^{-1}\bigg|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}\bigg(\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(x^{*},y^{*})-\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})+\gradient h(x^{*},y^{*})\bigg)_{1}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{(\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n})|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})|}_{T_{31}(i)}+\underbrace{M\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}\bigg|\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})\widehat{x}_{n}\bigg|}_{T_{32}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})(\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0})|}_{T_{33}(i)}+\underbrace{\bigg|\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}\bigg||(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}|}_{T_{34}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{M\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|x^{0}\|_{2}\absolutevalue{\hx^T\hSx\hx-(x^0)^T\hSx x^0-\hx^T\Sx\hx+(x^0)^T\Sx x^0}}_{T_{35}(i)}

We will provide some bounds on the
T_{2k}(i)
’s
(k=1,\ldots,5)
. The
O_{p}
terms appearing in the bounds do not depend on
i
, and depend only on
M
, and the Sub-gaussian norms of
X
and
Y
.

From Lemma
13.3
it follows that
\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}={\rho}_{0}+o_{p}(1)
. Using Lemma
13.15
we then obtain

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{31}(i)|\leq

\displaystyle\ \max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}O_{p}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i}\|_{2}O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|\Phi^{0}\|_{op}O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})

where (a) follows from Lemma
13.2
and (b) follows from Lemma
20.1
. Next, noting
\|\widehat{x}_{n}\|_{2}=O_{p}(1)
and
\|\widehat{x}_{n}\|_{1}=O_{p}(1)
by Lemma
13.2
, and using Lemma
13.12
, we obtain

|\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})\widehat{x}_{n}|=O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda).

Since Lemma
13.2
implies
\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)
, and Lemma
20.1
implies
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\leq\|\Phi^{0}\|_{op}=O(1)
, we have

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{32}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2}).

In the same way as we did for
T_{31}
,
We can deduce

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{33}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})
.

To control
T_{34}(i)
, first note that

\displaystyle\bigg|\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ |(\widehat{x}_{n}+x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})|+|(\widehat{x}_{n}+x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})|,

whose first term can be bounded by Lemma
13.15
and Lemma
13.2
to yield

|(\widehat{x}_{n}+x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})|\leq 2\|x^{0}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}O_{p}(\lambda)=O_{p}(s^{1/2+\kappa}\lambda^{2}),

and the second term can be bounded using Assumption
2.1
and Corollary
13.2
to yield

|(\widehat{x}_{n}+x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})|\leq M\|2x^{0}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda).

Because
s^{1/2}\lambda=o(1)
under Fact
1
,

\bigg|\widehat{x}_{n}^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}\widehat{x}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}x^{0}\bigg|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda).

On the other hand, another application of Lemma
13.15
yields

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}|=O_{p}(\lambda)\|x^{0}\|_{1}\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2},

which is
O_{p}(s^{1/2}\lambda)
by Lemma
13.7
and (
39
). Therefore

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{34}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{1/2+\kappa}\lambda^{2}).

For
T_{35}(i)
, note that Lemma
13.7
and (
39
) implies

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|x^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(1).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\hx^T\hSx\hx-(x^0)^T\hSx x^0-\hx^T\Sx\hx+(x^0)^T\Sx x^0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \absolutevalue{\hx^T(\hSx-\Sx)\hx-(x^0)^T(\hSx-\Sx)x^0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \absolutevalue{ (\hx-x^0)^T(\hSx-\Sx)\hx+ (x^0)^T(\hSx-\Sx)(\hx-x^0)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|x^{0}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}O_{p}(\lambda)+\|x^{0}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{1}O_{p}(\lambda)

where the last step follows from Lemma
13.15
. Using Lemma
13.2
, we conclude

\absolutevalue{\hx^T\hSx\hx-(x^0)^T\hSx x^0-\hx^T\Sx\hx+(x^0)^T\Sx x^0}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2}).

Thus
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{35}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})
as well.
Since we have shown that
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{k=1}^{5}|T_{3k}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})
, it then follows that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\bigg|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}\bigg(\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(x^{*},y^{*})-\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})+\gradient h(x^{*},y^{*})\bigg)_{1}\bigg|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2}).

Similarly we can show that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\bigg|(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}\bigg(\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\widehat{\gradient}h_{n}(x^{*},y^{*})-\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})+\gradient h(x^{*},y^{*})\bigg)_{2}\bigg|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2}),

which completes the proof of
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{3}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa+1/2}\lambda^{2})

15.6
Step 5: Showing
T_{4}(i)
is
O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2})

By Taylor series expansion, we obtain that

\displaystyle\gradient h(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})-\gradient h(x^{0},y^{0})=H(\widehat{\omega}_{n},\widehat{\vartheta}_{n})\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\\
\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\end{bmatrix}

where
(\widehat{\omega}_{n},\widehat{\vartheta}_{n})
is on the line joining
(x^{0},y^{0})
and

(\widehat{x}_{n},\widehat{y}_{n})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{k}\leq\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{k},\quad\|\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{k}\leq\|\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{k}\quad(k=1,2).

(43)

Therefore,

\displaystyle T_{4}(i)=

\displaystyle\ (\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}^{T}\Big(H(\widehat{\omega}_{n},\widehat{\vartheta}_{n})-H(x^{0},y^{0})\Big)\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\\
\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2\begin{bmatrix}(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}&(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{\omega}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}){\Sigma}_{x}&0\\
+2{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{\omega}_{n}\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}-x^{0}(x^{0})^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}&\\
0&(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-(y^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y^{0}){\Sigma}_{y}\\
&+2{\Sigma}_{y}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{T}-y^{0}(y^{0})^{T}){\Sigma}_{y}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\\
\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{(\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{\omega}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0})2(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})}_{T_{41}(i)}+4\underbrace{(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{\omega}_{n}\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}-x^{0}(x^{0})^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})}_{T_{42}(i)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-(y^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y^{0})2(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}(\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0})}_{T_{43}(i)}+\underbrace{4(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{T}-y^{0}(y^{0})^{T}){\Sigma}_{y}(\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0})}_{T_{44}(i)}

It suffices to show that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{41}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{42}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2}).

The proof of
T_{43}
and
T_{44}
will follow in a similar way, and hence will be skipped.

To control
T_{41}
, note that

\displaystyle|\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{\omega}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}|\leq

\displaystyle\ |\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{\omega}_{n}-x^{0})|+|(\widehat{\omega}_{n}-x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ M\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}(\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}\|_{2}+\|x^{0}\|_{2})

where the last step follows from Assumption
2.1
. From (
43
) and Lemma
13.2
, it follows that

|\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\widehat{\omega}_{n}-(x^{0})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}|=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda).

On the other hand, by Assumption
2.1
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}(\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0})|\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq p}M\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}

which is
O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)
by (
39
)
and Lemma
13.2
.
Therefore,

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}T_{41}(i)=O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2}).

For
T_{42}(i)
, note that by Assumption
2.1
,

\displaystyle|T_{4}(i)|\leq

\displaystyle\ 4M^{2}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}-x^{0}(x^{0})^{T}\|_{F}.

From (
39
) it follows that
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}=O(1)
and Lemma
13.2
entails that
\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)
. Fact
5
, on the other hand, implies that

\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}\widehat{\omega}_{n}^{T}-x^{0}(x^{0})^{T}\|_{F}\leq\|x^{0}\|_{2}^{-1}\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2},

which is
O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)
because
\|x^{0}\|_{2}\geq{\rho}_{0}M^{-1/2}
by Assumption
2.1
and
\|\widehat{\omega}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)
by (
43
) and Lemma
13.2
.
Therefore, it follows that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}T_{42}(i)=O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2}),

completing the proof of this step.

15.7
Step 5: Showing
T_{5}(i)
is
O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2})

T_{5}(i)
is bounded by

\displaystyle\|e_{i}-(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}^{T}H^{0}\|_{2}\Big(\|\widehat{x}_{n}-x^{0}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{y}_{n}-y^{0}\|_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \|(\Phi^{0}_{i}-(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i})^{T}H^{0}\|_{2}O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|\Phi^{0}_{i}-(\widehat{\Phi}_{n})_{i}\|_{2}\|H^{0}\|_{op}O_{p}(s^{\kappa}\lambda)

where (a) follows from Lemma
13.2
.
From Lemma
20.1
and (
42
)
it thus follows that
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|T_{5}(i)|=O_{p}(s^{2\kappa}\lambda^{2})
.

16
Proof of Proposition
4.3

First we state and prove a lemma that is key to proving Proposition
4.3
. This lemma establishes the joint asymptotic distribution of the random vector
\mathcal{L}
defined in (
14
) in terms of the
p+q
-variate Gaussian vector
\mathbb{Z}
appearing in the statement of Proposition
4.3
.

Lemma 16.1
.

Let
\Sigma_{z}
be the covariance matrix of
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{Z}(1),\ldots,\mathcal{Z}(p+q))
.
Under the set-up of Proposition
4.3
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the sub-Gaussian norms of
X
and
Y
, and the constants
M
and
\epsilon_{0}
, so that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{Z}\in A\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}

where
\mathbb{Z}
is a
p+q
-variate centered Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
\Sigma_{z}
.
Here
\epsilon_{0}
and
M
are as in Assumption
2.1
, and Assumption
2.1
, respectively.

Proof 16.2
(of Lemma
16.1
).

Note that for
i=1,\ldots,p+q
,

\displaystyle 2^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{i}=

\displaystyle\ {\rho}_{0}(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}+{\rho}_{0}(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}-(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})y^{0}

\displaystyle\ -(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})x^{0}+\underbrace{((\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0})}_{\xi_{1}(i)}(x^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,x}-{\Sigma}_{x})x^{0}

\displaystyle\ +(\underbrace{(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y^{0}}_{\xi_{2}(i)})(y^{0})^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,y}-{\Sigma}_{y})y^{0}

Observe that
E\mathcal{L}_{i}=0
. moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{i}=2n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}(i)-E\mathcal{Z}_{j}(i))
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{j}(i)=

\displaystyle\ ({\rho}_{0}(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}+\xi_{1}(i)(x^{0})^{T})X_{j}X_{j}^{T}x^{0}+({\rho}_{0}(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}+\xi_{2}(i)(y^{0})^{T})Y_{j}Y_{j}^{T}y^{0}

\displaystyle\ -(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}X_{j}Y_{j}^{T}y^{0}-(x^{0})^{T}X_{j}Y_{j}^{T}\Phi^{0}_{i,2}.

Let us consider the
p+q
variate
iid random vectors
\mathcal{Z}_{j}=(\mathcal{Z}_{j}(i))_{1\leq i\leq p+q}

(j=1,\ldots,n)
, which are iid copies of
\mathcal{Z}
. Note that we can express
\mathcal{L}
in terms of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
’s since
\mathcal{L}=2n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}-E[\mathcal{Z}_{j}])
.
We intend to use a Berry-Esseen type theorem. In particular, we apply
Theorem 2.1 of
Chernozhukov et al. 2017
.
Note that we can express
\mathcal{L}
in terms of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
’s since
\mathcal{L}/2=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}-E[\mathcal{Z}_{j}])
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the set of all hyperrectangles in
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
.
Theorem 2.1 of
Chernozhukov et al. 2017
states that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}-E[\mathcal{Z}_{j}])\in A\Big)-P\Big(\mathbb{Z}\in A\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{C_{z}^{2}\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}

(44)

provided

A1.

There exists
c>0
so that
\min\limits_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\sigma_{i}^{2}>c
where
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\text{var}(\mathcal{Z}(i))
.

A2.

There exists
C_{z}>0
so that

E\Big[|\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]|^{3}\Big]\leq C_{z},\quad E\Big[\Big(\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]\Big)^{4}\Big]\leq C_{z}^{2}\quad i=1,\ldots,(p+q).

A3.

The
C_{z}
in A3 also satisfies

\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E\Big[\exp\Big(C_{z}^{-1}\left|\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]\right|\Big)\Big]\leq 2.

A1 follows from our assumption on the
\sigma_{i}^{2}
’s. To prove A2,
first we will bound
\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)|^{3}]
and
\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)|^{4}]
, which is not immediate since the moment expressions of the
\mathcal{Z}(i)
’s involve
p
and
q
dimensional vectors.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{S}^{p+q-1}
the unit
l_{2}
ball in
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
.
Note that for
a,b\in\mathcal{S}^{p+q-1}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle E[|a^{T}XX^{T}b|^{k}]\leq\bigg(E[(a^{T}X_{1})^{2k}]E[(b^{T}X_{1})^{2k}]\bigg)^{1/2}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Because
X
is a sub-Gaussian random vector,
a^{T}X
is a sub-Gaussian variable, which implies
(
Vershynin 2010
, cf. (5.11) of )

E[|a^{T}X|^{2k}]\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2k}(2k)^{k},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}
is the sub-Gaussian norm
(
Vershynin 2010
, cf. Definition 5.7 of )
. Note that
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty
because
X
is sub-Gaussian.
Thus,

E[|a^{T}XX^{T}b|^{k}]\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2k}(2k)^{k}.

Thus for
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\displaystyle E|a^{T}XX^{T}b|^{k}\leq\|X\|^{2k}_{\psi_{2}}(2k)^{k}\|a\|_{2}^{k}\|b\|_{2}^{k}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(45)

Similarly, for
a\in\mathcal{S}^{p-1}
and
b\in\mathcal{S}^{q-1}
, we can show that

E|a^{T}XY^{T}b|^{k}\leq(2k)^{k}\bigg(\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2k}\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2k}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Therefore, for
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we can show that

E|a^{T}XY^{T}b|^{k}\leq(2k)^{k}\bigg(\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2k}\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2k}\bigg)^{1/2}\|a\|_{2}^{k}\|b\|_{2}^{k}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(46)

Moreover, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a universal constant
c>0
so that

(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}|)^{3}\leq c\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}|^{3}\quad\text{and}\quad(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}|)^{4}\leq c\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}|^{4}.

Hence, there exists
C>0
depending only on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
and
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
so that

\displaystyle E|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)|^{k}\leq

\displaystyle\ C\bigg\{\|x^{0}\|_{2}^{k}\Big(\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}^{k}+|\xi_{1}(i)|^{k}\|x^{0}\|_{2}^{k}\Big)+\|y^{0}\|_{2}^{k}\Big(\|\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}^{k}+|\xi_{2}(i)|^{k}\|y^{0}\|_{2}^{k}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}^{k}\|y^{0}\|_{2}^{k}+\|\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}^{k}\|x^{0}\|_{2}^{k}\bigg\}\quad k=3,4,

where we used the fact that
{\rho}_{0}\leq 1
. Lemma
13.7
implies
\|x^{0}\|_{2}
and
\|y^{0}\|_{2}
bounded above by a constant. On the other hand, (
39
) implies

\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\max\{\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2},\|\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}\}\leq\frac{M}{2\epsilon_{0}}.

These facts also imply
|\xi_{1}(i)|
and
|\xi_{2}(i)|
are bounded. To see this, note that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}|\xi_{1}(i)|\leq\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}x^{0}\|_{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\leq M^{1/2}\|\Phi^{0}\|_{op},

(47)

which is bounded above.
Similarly, we can show that
|\xi_{2}(i)|
is bounded uniformly over
i=1,\ldots,p+q
. Thus, we conclude that there exists
C>0
depending only on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
M
, and
{\rho}_{0}-\Lambda_{2}
so that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)|^{3}],\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)|^{4}]\leq C.

Hence,
E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)^{2}]
is also bounded by
C
uniformly across
i=1,\ldots,p+q
. Since

E\Big[(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)])^{3}\Big]=E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)^{3}]-3E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)^{2}]E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]+2E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]^{3},

E\Big[(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)])^{4}\Big]=E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)^{4}]-4E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)^{3}]E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]+6E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)^{2}]E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]^{2}-3E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]^{4},

it follows that there exists
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
M
, and
\epsilon_{0}
so that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E\Big[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]|^{3}\Big],\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E\Big[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]|^{4}\Big]\leq C^{\prime}.

(48)

Let us denote
C_{z}^{\prime}=\max(C^{\prime},1)
. It is easy to see that
C_{z}
satisfies A2 if
C_{z}>C_{z}^{\prime}
.

Next, we will find the moment generating functions of
|\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]|
, using which we will choose a
C_{z}
that satisfies A2 and A3. First of all, note that since
X
and
Y
are sub-Gaussian,
a^{T}X
and
b^{T}Y
are sub-Gaussian.
Since the product of sub-Gaussian random variables is sub-exponential
(
Vershynin 2018
, cf. Lemma 2.7.5 of )
, and sum of sub-exponential random variables is also sub-exponential
(
Vershynin 2018
, cf. Bernstein inequality, Theorem 2.8.2 of )
,
\mathcal{Z}(i)
is also sub-exponential.

The sub-exponential norm
\|Z\|_{\psi_{1}}
of a sub-Gaussian random variable
Z
is defined by
(
Vershynin 2018
, cf. Definition 2.7.3 of)

\|Z\|_{\psi_{1}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:E[\exp(|Z|/t)]\leq 2\}.

Therefore, for
t\geq\|\mathcal{Z}(i)\|_{\psi_{1}}
, we have

E[\exp({|Z|/t})]\leq 2
. This implies if
C_{z}
satisfies

C_{z}\geq\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\|\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]\|_{\psi_{1}},

(49)

then
C_{z}
satisfies A3 as well.
Now

\|\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\|\mathcal{Z}(i)\|_{\psi_{1}}+|E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]|

(50)

Fact
8
implies that there exists a constant
C
depending on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
and
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
so that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\|\mathcal{Z}(i)\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq

\displaystyle\ C\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\bigg\{\|x^{0}\|_{2}\Big(\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}+|\xi_{1}(i)|\|x^{0}\|_{2}\Big)+\|y^{0}\|_{2}\Big(\|\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}+|\xi_{2}(i)|\|y^{0}\|_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\|\Phi^{0}_{i,1}\|_{2}\|y^{0}\|_{2}+\|\Phi^{0}_{i,2}\|_{2}\|x^{0}\|_{2}\bigg\}.

(51)

The fact that the right hand side is bounded follows from Lemma
13.7
, (
39
), and (
47
), Assumption
2.1
. As in the proof of A1, it can also be shown that the bound depends only on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
M
, and the
\epsilon_{0}
in Assumption
2.1
.
On the other hand,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]=

\displaystyle\ {\rho}_{0}((\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}+(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}y^{0})+{\rho}_{0}(\xi_{1}(i)+\xi_{2}(i))

\displaystyle\ -(\Phi^{0}_{i,1})^{T}{\Sigma}_{xy}y^{0}-(\Phi^{0}_{i,2})^{T}{\Sigma}_{yx}x^{0}.

Since
{\Sigma}_{xy}y^{0}={\rho}_{0}{\Sigma}_{x}x^{0}
and
{\Sigma}_{yx}x^{0}={\rho}_{0}{\Sigma}_{y}y^{0}
, we have

\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]={\rho}_{0}\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}(\xi_{1}(i)+\xi_{2}(i))<M^{1/2}\|\Phi^{0}\|_{op},

where the last step follows by (
47
). Combining the above with (
50
) and (
16.2
) implies that
\max_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\|\mathcal{Z}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)]\|_{\psi_{1}}
can be bounded by some
D_{z}^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
M
, and
\epsilon_{0}
. Therefore, according to (
49
),
C_{z}
satisfies A3 if
C_{z}>D_{z}^{\prime}
. Recall that we showed that
C_{z}
satisfies A2 if
C_{z}>C_{z}^{\prime}
for some
C_{z}^{\prime}=\max(C^{\prime},1)
where
C^{\prime}
is defined in (
48
). Therefore, if
C_{z}>\max(D_{z}^{\prime},C_{z}^{\prime})
, then A2 and A3 holds. Suppose
t=(t_{i})_{1\leq i\leq p+q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
. Since the constant
C_{z}
does not depend on
p
,
q
, or
n
,
(
44
) implies there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\|X\|_{psi_{2}}
,
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
M
and
\epsilon_{0}
so that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}-E[\mathcal{Z}_{j}])\in A\Big)-P\Big(\mathbb{Z}\in A\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}.

Since
A\in\mathcal{A}
implies
2A\in\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{i}=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}(i)])/n
, we have

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{Z}\in A\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}.

In particular, for
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{p+q})
, we have

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{i}\leq t_{i},\ 1\leq i\leq p+q\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{Z}_{i}\leq t_{i},\ 1\leq i\leq p+q\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}.

Hence the proof follows.

Proof 16.3
(of Proposition
4.3
).

Since
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
consists of the first
p
co-ordinates of
\mathcal{L}
,
from Lemma
16.1
, we obtain that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6},

where
\mathbb{X}=(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
consists of the first
p
co-ordinates of
\mathbb{Z}
, a
p+q
-variate centred Gaussian random vector. Since the covariance of
\mathbb{Z}
is the covariance matrix of the
p+q
-variate random vector
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{Z}(i))_{1\leq i\leq p+q}
, it follows that the covariance matrix of
\mathbb{X}
is the covariance matrix of
(\mathcal{Z}(1),\ldots,\mathcal{Z}(p))
, which we denoted by

\Sigma_{p}
.

Theorem
4.1
implies that either

\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0}=-\mathcal{L}_{(1)}+\text{rem}\quad\text{or}\quad\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}+x^{0}=-\mathcal{L}_{(1)}+\text{rem}.

Suppose the former holds. Then

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0}-\text{rem})\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}.

(52)

Hence

\displaystyle\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0})\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0}-\text{rem})\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\ +\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0})\in A\Big)-P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0}-\text{rem})\in A\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}+\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}P\bigg(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0}-\text{rem})\in A-\text{rem},n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0}-\text{rem})\in A^{c}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}+\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}P\underbrace{\bigg(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in A-\text{rem},-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in A^{c}\bigg)}_{\mathcal{E}(A)}

(53)

For any set
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\epsilon>0
, we denote

D(A,\epsilon)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\text{there exists }y\in A\text{ so that }\|x-y\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\}.

Note that

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{E}(A))\leq P\bigg(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\setminus A\bigg)

Since
A
is a hyperrectangle in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})
is also a hyperrectangle in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\bigg(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\setminus A\bigg)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\setminus A\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\bigg(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\bigg)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\ +\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\bigg(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in A\bigg)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\ 2\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\bigg(-n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\in A\bigg)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|,

which is bounded by
C(\log^{7}((p+q)n)/n)^{1/6}
by (
52
). Here (a) follows because
D(A,\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty})\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}P(\mathcal{E}(A))\leq C\bigg(\frac{\log^{7}((p+q)n)}{n}\bigg)^{1/6}+\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in D(A,\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty})\setminus A\Big).

(54)

Let us consider a particular
A
. Any
A
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
has the form
A=[x_{1},y_{1}]\times\ldots[x_{p+q},y_{p+q}]
where
-\infty\leq x_{i}<\infty
, and
-\infty<y_{i}\leq\infty(i=1,\ldots,p+q)
.
Some algebra leads to

\displaystyle P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\setminus A\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq p}P\Big(x_{i}-\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq x_{i}\Big)

(55)

\displaystyle+\max_{1\leq i\leq p}P\Big(y_{i}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq y_{i}+\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\Big).

(56)

Note that if either
x_{i}=-\infty
or
y_{i}=\infty
, then

P\Big(x_{i}-\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq x_{i}\Big)=0\quad\text{or}\quad P\Big(y_{i}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq y_{i}+\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\Big)=0.

(57)

For any
\epsilon>0
, we calculate

\displaystyle P\Big(x_{i}-\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq x_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ P(\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\geq x_{i}-2\mathbb{X}_{i},\ x_{i}-2\mathbb{X}_{i}\geq\epsilon)+P(0\leq x_{i}-2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ P(\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon)+\displaystyle\int_{x_{i}-\epsilon}^{x_{i}}\frac{e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{8\sigma_{i}^{2}}}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}2\sigma_{i}}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ P(\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon)+\frac{C\epsilon}{\min\limits_{1\leq i\leq p}\sigma_{i}},

where
C
is an absolute constant not depending on
x_{i}
. Our assumptions imply that there exists
c>0
so that
c\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq p}\sigma_{i}
. This, combined with (
57
), implies that there exists
C>0
so that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}P\Big(x_{i}-\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq x_{i}\Big)\leq P(\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon)+C\epsilon.

Similarly, we can show that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}P\Big(y_{i}\leq 2\mathbb{X}_{i}\leq y_{i}+\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\Big)\leq P(\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon)+C\epsilon.

Equation
55
implies that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\setminus A\Big)\leq P(\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon)+C\epsilon

Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, and
\|\text{rem}\|_{\infty}=o_{p}(1)
, it follows that as
n\to\infty
,

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in D(A,\|rem\|_{\infty})\setminus A\Big)\to 0,

which, combined with (
16.3
), (
54
), and the fact that
\log p=o(n^{-1/7})
implies

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}-x^{0})\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|\to 0

as
n\to\infty
. If
\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}+x^{0}=-\mathcal{L}_{(1)}+\text{rem}
, we can similarly show that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bigg|P\Big(n^{1/2}(\widehat{x}^{db}_{n}+x^{0})\in A\Big)-P\Big(2\mathbb{X}\in A\Big)\bigg|\to 0.

17
Proof of Corollaries in Section
4

Proof 17.1
(of Corollary
4.2
).

Equation
48
implies that under Assumption
2.1
and
2.1
, there exists
C>0
depending only on
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}
,
M
, and
{\rho}_{0}-\Lambda_{2}
so that

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}E\Big[|\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)-E[\mathcal{Z}_{1}(i)]|^{4}\Big]\leq C.

Therefore
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\text{var}(\mathcal{Z})
is also finite.
Letting

s_{n}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\text{var}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})=n\text{var}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=n\sigma^{2}_{i},

we note

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{E|\mathcal{Z}_{j}-E\mathcal{Z}_{j}|^{4}}{s_{n}^{4}}\leq\frac{nC}{n^{2}\sigma_{i}^{4}}=O\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\bigg).

Hence,
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
’s satisfy the Lyaponov’s condition
(
Billingsley 2008
, cf. Theorem 27.3 of)
. Therefore

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}-E\mathcal{Z}_{i})}{s_{n}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{i}-E\mathcal{Z}_{i})}{(n\sigma_{i}^{2})^{1/2}}\to_{d}N(0,1),

which implies
n^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{i}
converges in distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
4\sigma_{i}^{2}
.
Therefore Theorem
4.1
implies that
\widehat{x}^{db}_{n,i}
satisfies either
